Bigelow and Poremba (2014) controlled for this confound by using only temporally-dynamic stimuli, and found auditory recognition was still poorer than both visual and tactile recognition.

However, this relative deficit is not constant across all test formats and stimulus modalities. For example, environmental sounds are remembered as well or better than their verbal labels in free recall, but the sound deficit reappears with serial free recall (Paivio, 1975; Philipchalk & Rowe, 1971; Warren, 1969; see also Visscher et al., 2007). Although this pattern has been replicated multiple times, it has also been shown that cued recall of visual information is better than auditory when more complex and ecologically-valid materials are used (Huss & Weaver, 1996).

So, although environmental sounds can be remembered better than other materials, this appears to be restricted to a free recall of sounds vs. their labels to date, and that pattern can be explained by differences in elaborative encoding of the two stimulus types (Paivio et al., 1975). Thus, there appears to be some fundamental differences between these modalities in long-term memory, which are perhaps most consistently found when comparing sounds and pictures in recognition (Cohen et al., 2009 (Cohen et al., , 2011;; Gloede et al., 2017; Gloede & Gregg, 2019; for a review see Snyder & Gregg, 2011).

However, we have a limited understanding of the specific ways in which they differ. It could be that auditory objects are inherently less memorable (e.g., Cohen et al., 2009) or that memory capacity is simply lower for environmental sounds (Snyder & Gregg, 2011). However, these accounts seem to describe the results rather than explain them. They also do not seem to fit with findings of similar shortterm memory abilities in the two modalities (e.g., Hawkins et al., 2025; Visscher et al., 2007; for a review see Snyder & Gregg, 2011).

It is possible that explanations of the picture superiority effect (PSE) can also explain the visualauditory difference. Distinctiveness of the stimuli is at least part of the cause of the PSE, given that it can be eliminated when words are made as distinctive as the pictures (Ensor et al., 2019). This is appealing as an explanation of the audio-visual difference, but investigations to date have failed to support it (e.g., Ahmad et al., 2024), and it does not account for similarities in short-term memory performance (Snyder & Gregg, 2011).

Other candidates for explanations have recently been investigated, including differences in the precision of representations (Gloede et al., 2017; Gloede & Gregg, 2019) and the role of gist and verbatim information (Ahmad et al., 2024). Gloede and Gregg (2019) found that the participants' percent-correct scores were better for rejecting similar lures of visual than auditory objects on an immediate recognition test, suggesting differences in the type and/or specificity of detail encoded and/or retrieved. Ahmad et al. (2024) also investigated modality differences in recognition memory with a similar-lures paradigm, and found that both verbatim and gist memory were better for pictures than sounds. In contrast, Zhang et al. (2026) argued that auditory recognition is more gist-based than visual recognition, based on fMRI evidence that auditory recognition is correlated with more "internally transformed" representations. Overall, these findings point to the idea that visual memory may retain more fine detail than does auditory memory.

One thing missing from this line of research is the application of a theoretically-driven measurement model. Here, we take a dual process (Jacoby, 199; Mandler, 1980) approach to measuring recognition, using the Dual Process Signal Detection (DPSD) model (Yonelinas, 1994; Yonelinas, 2002) to obtain independent estimates of recollection (the retrieval of contextual detail) and familiarity (a decontextualized feeling of prior exposure). Until 1991, these processes were difficult to measure in accord with dual-process assumptions (e.g., that recollection and familiarity are independent). Although the remember-know paradigm was introduced by Tulving in 1985, it assumes mutual exclusivity of recollection and familiarity (unless the estimates are corrected, Yonelinas & Jacoby, 1995). Jacoby's (1991) measurement model, the process dissociation procedure, provided a way to estimate recollection and familiarity independently. This was a watershed moment that generated a great deal of research, resulting in a wealth of knowledge about various aspects of cognitive control and automaticity.

For example, the debates about the model's assumptions, such as independence (e.g., Curran & Hintzman, 1995; Jacoby, 1998), proved fruitful for aging research (showing declines in recollection, but not familiarity, Jennings & Jacoby, 1993), as well as understanding other standard manipulations of memory (e.g., generate-read, Jacoby, 1991; Jennings & Jacoby, 1993; divided attention, Jacoby & Kelly, 1991; Yonelinas, 2001; levels of processing, Yonelinas, 2001). Following Jacoby's example, in this paper we first examine whether recollection and familiarity differ for visual and auditory objects and then use an attention manipulation to undermine recollection specifically. Our goal was to use the dual-process theory to look under the hood of the audio-visual modality effect on recognition memory to see whether (and how) it affects recollection and familiarity.

We approached the first experiment in an exploratory way, simply asking how the recognition difference for sounds and pictures is reflected in estimates of recollection and familiarity, which might suggest that one of these memory processes plays a larger role in explaining the modality difference.

Specifically, if the modality effect is larger in recollection than familiarity, it would suggest that encoding and/or retrieval of contextual detail is particularly important for understanding the modality effect.

Some evidence already points in that direction (e.g., Ahmad et al., 2024; Gloede & Gregg, 2019), but those studies did not measure recollection and familiarityfoot_0 . On the other hand, if modality has no effect on the encoding and/or retrieval of contextual information, then the difference might be found primarily in familiarity.

We tested these hypotheses in the first experiment by simply manipulating modality within participants in a study-test block for each modality (counterbalanced for order). We expected that if modality had a larger effect on one process or the other, it would be larger for recollection. Code, data, supplementary materials, programs, and preregistrations for Experiments 3 and 4 can be found at:

Method

Participants

Participants were 30 University of Nevada, Las Vegas (UNLV) students (M age = 21.0, 22 women) recruited from the Psychology Department's subject pool who participated in return for course credit.

Three additional participants' data were excluded from analysis for having used too few responses to construct a receiver operating characteristic upon which to base the d a measure (n = 2) and for poor performance (d a < .20, n = 1) 2 . No power analysis was performed because there were no previous experiments on which to base such an analysis; we based our sample size on older textbooks' rule of thumb for the central limit theorem, which was that a sample will approach a normal distribution for n ≥ 30, thereby meeting assumptions for the use of t-tests (e.g., Hays, 1994). All experiments reported here were approved by the UNLV IRB. This experiment was not preregistered.

Materials

We used 160 2-s auditory objects (e.g., a dog bark) taken from Norman-Haignere et al. (2015). These were primarily environmental sounds, but also included some music and speech clips.

Images (160) were colored line drawings taken from Rossion and Pourtois (2004) 3 . Study and test trials were blocked by modality (all of one modality at a time), and block order was counterbalanced across participants. Study lists consisted of 80 items each, and two buffer items preceded and ended the study lists. All study items were presented at test, as well as 80 new items. Items were randomly assigned to old/new status for each subject. We used E-Prime 2.0 to program the experiments.

Procedure

After providing informed consent, participants read instructions for the first study task, which were reiterated by the experimenter. Participants were instructed to memorize each item as best they could for a later memory test, and they were told that trying to identify (or label) each item would help them to remember it. Auditory objects were presented for 2-s each, with a 2.5-s interstimulus interval (ISI). The computer screen was blank during the auditory encoding task. Visual objects were presented at a rate of 700 ms, with a 1-s ISI. The visual items were presented at a faster rate than the auditory items to avoid ceiling effects. The auditory encoding phase took 12 min and the visual study phase took 4.5 min.

Instructions for the recognition test followed each study task; participants were asked to use a 6-point response scale to make old/new decisions about each item, with 1 anchored as "Sure New" and 6 anchored as "Sure Old". Participants were strongly encouraged to use the whole scale across the course of each test. At test, sounds were played for the full 2s before participants were able to respond and pictures were displayed for the full 700ms before they could respond, matching the duration times during the study phase. Once the stimulus was presented, it was removed from the screen or silenced, but the confidence scale remained onscreen and participants responded at their own pace. Modality was manipulated within participants, and study-test block order was counterbalanced across participants.

Measurements and Analyses

We used the ttestBF and anovaBF functions of the BayesFactor package in R (Morey & Rouder, 2024; R Core Team, 2021) as well as the effectsize package (Ben-Shachar et al., 2020) for Cohen's d, and for ƞ p 2 . We used the bayestestR::bayesfactor_inclusion() function from the bayestestR (Markowski et al., 2019) package to calculate inclusion BFs. We used the BF guidelines as suggested by Lee & Wagenmakers' (2013) adaptation of Jeffreys' scale. Programs (E-Prime), data, and R code are posted on the Open Science Framework website (https://osf.io/va3ck/overview).

We measured overall recognition with d a (Macmillan & Creelman, 2005) and used the Dual Process Signal Detection model (Yonelinas, 1994) to calculate estimates of recollection and familiarity from confidence-based receiver operating characteristics (ROCs; Yonelinas & Parks, 2007). Like Jacoby's (1991) model, DPSD treats recollection as a threshold process (meaning it can fail entirely). However, one limitation of Jacoby's original model was that it also treated familiarity as a threshold process (i.e., a probability). Yonelinas (1994) modified the test procedure by using confidence judgments in old/new responses. This allowed the model to better reflect familiarity as a continuous signal detection process (familiarity cannot fail, because all stimuli will evoke some familiarity signal, no matter how small).

Findings in behavioral, lesion, and neuroimaging research have supported this model repeatedly (for reviews, see Yonelinas et al., 2024; Yonelinas & Parks, 2007; Yonelinas, 2002; see Wixted, 2007 for alternative interpretations).

Our goal was to compare the size of the modality effect on recollection and familiarity. Because recollection and familiarity are on different scales, they cannot be directly compared to one another (recollection is a probability and familiarity is d', which is unbounded). Therefore, we conducted analyses (Bayesian ANOVAs and t-tests) separately for the two parameters and compared effect sizes and Bayes factors (BF) to infer whether the manipulations affected one process more than the other (effect sizes included ƞ p 2 for ANOVA and Cohen's d for t-tests and their 95% confidence intervals) 4 . We report all BFs as BF10 , which is evidence in favor of the alternative hypothesisfoot_4 . We used test order (auditory or visual first) as a between-subjects variable in the first round of analyses; we found that order had a small effect on two dependent measures in Experiment 3, but did not change any conclusions. Therefore, we report the analyses without test order included. Analyses with test order are available for each experiment on OSF: https://osf.io/va3ck/overview

Results and Discussion

Visual inspection of the receiver operating characteristics (Figure 1) indicates differences in recognition, recollection, and familiarity. Paired t-tests showed that there was a large difference in overall recognition (d a ) between auditory and visual modalities (BF10 = 2250.25, Cohen's d = .99, 95% CI [.54, 1.43]), replicating the typical finding of visual memory superiority. More importantly, recollection was better in the visual than the auditory condition (BF10 = 284.17, Cohen's d = 1.06, 95% CI [0.48, 1.64]), and though this was also true for familiarity, both the effect size and the evidence were weaker (BF10 = 1.89, Cohen's d = .53, 95% CI [0.04, 1.01]). Thus, the modality effect on recollection was larger than on familiarity, as we suspected it might be. Note that the time it took to complete the study phase differed between the conditions, but we chose to continue with this design as a means of keeping visual recognition below ceiling. We return to this issue in the General Discussion. and Experiment 2 as a function of attention and modality. function of modality and attention. Exp. = Experiment. Exp. 1 included only a manipulation of modality.

Exp. 2 -4 included an additional manipulation of attention. FA = Full attention and DA = Divided attention. Modality had a consistently larger effect on recollection than familiarity, and there was a larger effect of attention on visual than auditory recollection. Note that the process estimates are on different scales. Bars represent one standard error of the mean, and the dots overlaying the bars represent individual subject's data points.

Experiment 2: Attention x Modality

Experiment 1 replicated the common finding that recognition is better for pictures than for sounds, and that the same pattern can be seen in both recollection and familiarity estimates. However, modality had a bigger effect on recollection than familiarity, which indicates that participants have less access to qualitative detail about environmental sounds than pictures, which could be due to encoding and/or retrieval factors. These results align with previous findings that discriminating between old, new, and similar-lure items is easier in the visual than the auditory modality (Gloede et al., 2017), which also points to more difficulty in accessing detailed representations. Thus, we focused next on recollection, by undermining the ability to encode (and/or retrieve) qualitative detail. To do this, we interleaved an nback task with the stimuli during the study phases to divide attention. We expected that divided attention would reduce recollection in both modalities and have smaller effects on familiarity, with the open question being whether dividing attention has a different effect in the two modalities.

Method

Participants

Sixty participants (Mage = 18.8, 41 women, 19 men) from the UNLV Psychology Department's subject pool took part in this experiment in return for course credit. An additional five participants' data were excluded from analyses for not using enough of the scale to form an ROC (n = 1), for not making any misses in the visual condition (n = 2), for a computer crash (n = 1), and for only using "6" responses to visual items (n = 1). Because credible evidence of differences was found in Experiment 1 with 30 participants, we used that sample size for this experiment as well, with 30 participants per full and divided conditions.

Materials

The materials were the same as those used in Experiment 1, with the addition of an n-back task with numbers between 1 and 9. The task was a 2-back task, presented in the modality opposite of the stimuli being encoded; thus, the n-back task was presented in black font in the middle of the computer screen when participants were studying auditory items, and was presented over headphones when studying the visual objects. When the 2-back was auditory, the spoken number words were read in a computer-generated male voice with an American accent.

Procedure

The procedure for the full attention condition was the same as in Experiment 1. Attention was divided in a second group of participants with a 2-back task. Between the presentation of each target stimulus, a number was presented and participants had to decide whether it was the same as two numbers back or not. The number stimuli were presented in the modality opposite of the studied stimuli. Thus, participants in the divided attention group were presented with an object to be remembered, followed by a number with the response options same or different for the n-back task (entered via keyboard), another object, and so on. Participants had three seconds to respond on n-back trials (via keyboard presses) in both the auditory and visual study phases, and they practiced the task before starting the first study phase. If the participant did not respond within 3s, the program moved on to the next to-be-remembered object. Participants were told it was very important to perform well on the n-back task, and that they should also memorize the objects for a later memory test. The total time of the study phases differed by attention condition and modality. Full attention auditory took 12 min and full attention visual took 4.5 minutes. Total study time in the divided attention conditions varied per participant due to response times to the 2-back task. However, if they took the maximum amount of time to respond to the 2-back trials, total study time would have been 20 min for the auditory condition and 12.5 min in the visual condition. Participants were randomly assigned to either the full or divided attention condition, and modality remained a within-subjects variable. There was no change to the test, nor to the study or test instructions, save for the explanation of the n-back task in the divided attention condition. All ANOVAs were 2 (modality) x 2 (attention) mixed ANOVA models, with attention as a between-subjects variable and modality as a within-subjects variable.

Analyses

In addition to presenting the best fitting ANOVA model, we also report the degree to which it is better than the second-best model. The Inclusion Bayes Factors are also included (see Table 2). These were calculated in two ways: across the full model space (i.e., match_models = TRUE, indicating a consideration of all combinations of predictors), and again with match_models = FALSE, such that each effect is compared to others with only that effect term and its higher order interactions. This allows interaction terms to be evaluated independently of their main effects. We used this approach for all ANOVAs in the following experiments.

Results and Discussion

Accuracy and RTs are presented in Table 1 for Experiments 2 -4. Evidence of a difference in accuracy on the n-back tasks in the different study modality conditions was inconclusive (BF10 = .90, Cohen's d = .35, 95% CI [-0.04, 0.73]). However, RTs did differ, with participants responding slower in the auditory study condition than in the visual study condition (BF10 = 80.36, d = .86, 95% CI [0.36, 1.36]).

Note that the n-back was presented visually during encoding of sounds, and presented aurally during encoding of pictures. For d a , the best ANOVA model included both main effects of modality and attention, as well as their interaction (Figure 2 Exp 2, BF10 = 5.95e+11, interaction ƞ p2 = .32, 95% CI [0.14, 0.49]). This model was 452.16 times more likely than the second-best model that did not include the main effect of modality; inclusion factors can be found in Table 2. Bayesian t-tests revealed decisive evidence of a large effect of attention on visual d a (BF10 = 5,262,639, d = 1.85, 95% CI [1.23, 2.47]) and a smaller effect that was strongly supported on auditory d a (BF10 = 48.31, d = .94, 95% CI [.39, 1.48]).

For recollection, the best ANOVA model included both main effects and the interaction (BF10 = 1.24e+9, interaction ƞ p2 = .31, 95% CI [0.13, 0.48]). This model was 1,292.94 times more likely than the second-best model, which did not include a main effect of attention. Surprisingly, two-tailed independent-samples t-tests showed a decisively large effect of divided attention on visual recollection (BF10 = 317,671.40, Cohen's d = 1.65, 95% CI [1.05, 2.25]), and substantial evidence in favor of a null effect on auditory recollection (BF10 = .29, Cohen's d = .13, 95% CI [-0.39, 0.65]). Thus, dividing attention affected visual but not auditory recollection.

For familiarity, the best ANOVA model included the main effect of attention and the attention x modality interaction (BF10 = 55,994.28, interaction ƞ p2 = .12, 95% CI [0.01, 0.28]). This model was 1.35 times more likely than the second-best model. Attention had a somewhat larger effect on visual (BF10 = 1845.96, d = 1.26, 95% CI [0.69, 1.82]) than auditory familiarity (BF10 = 207.26, d = 1.07, 95% CI [0.52, 1.63]).

Experiment 2 replicated the results of Experiment 1. When looking specifically at the full attention condition, modality affected recollection more than familiarity (modality effect Cohen's d is 1.54 for recollection, and .48 for familiarity). Unexpectedly, we found that dividing attention had no detectable effect on auditory recollection but had a large effect on visual recollection (Cohen's d = .13 and d = 1.65, respectively). Dividing attention also reduced familiarity, more so than one would expect based on prior work (Yonelinas, 2002). the interpretable probability that the term is included. BF incl (averaged) averages over all models; BF incl (matched) compares only models differing by that term, excluding models containing its higherorder interactions. The two BF columns diverge when a term is involved in a supported interaction; in that case the matched value's posterior mass can fall below its prior, which is not evidence against the term. Participant was included in all models and is excluded here for space. Original tables can be found in the supplementary information on OSF: https://osf.io/va3ck/overview.

Experiment 3: Attention Modality x Stimulus Modality

The null effect of attention on auditory recollection was surprising enough that we tested it again with a new sample, and manipulated the modality of the divided attention task to make sure that it was unimportant for the results we saw in Experiment 2. To do this, we tested three groups: full attention, divided attention with the n-back task in the modality opposite of the study items (as in Experiment 2), and divided attention with n-back in the same modality, resulting in a 3 (attention: full, divided-same, divided-different) x 2 (modality) mixed design, with modality as the within-subjects variable and attention as the between-subjects variable. We expected to replicate the findings of Experiment 2, and to see no differences between the two divided attention tasks. The preregistration can be found at: https://osf.io/wu7qt/overview

Method

Participants

We used the recollection effect size from Experiment 2 to conduct a power analysis, which indicated a sample of 78, with 26 participants in each condition. Seventy-eight participants (M age = 19.4, 52 women, 26 men) from the UNLV Psychology Department's subject pool took part in this experiment in return for course credit. Several additional participants completed the experiment, but their data were excluded from analyses (using preregistered criteria): three for not using enough of the confidence scale to form an ROC, five for not doing the DA task, three for failing to use the response scale correctly, and five for low performance (d a < .20).

Materials and Procedure

Experiment 3 was the same as Experiment 2, but included an additional condition in which the n-back task was presented in the same modality as the study stimuli.

Results and Discussion

Accuracy was similar across the divided attention tasks (see Table 1). The best-supported ANOVA model for the alternative hypothesis included only the modality x attention interaction (BF10 = .53, interaction ƞ p2 = .03, 95% CI [0.00, 0.18]), and there was strong evidence against the full model (BF10 = .04). The modality x attention interaction model was 1.63 times more likely than the second-best model, which included only the main effect of attention (BF10 = .32). In contrast, the best model for RT included the main effect of attention and the modality x attention interaction (BF10 = 4,062,850, interaction ƞ p2 = .50, 95% CI [0.30, 0.64]). This model was 1.56 times more likely than the next best model, which included only the interaction. RTs in the visual study condition were faster when the divided attention task was also visual. In the auditory condition, RTs were faster when the task was in the opposite modality.

As can be seen in Figure 2 (Exp 3), the recognition memory results were similar to the previous experiments. The best 3 (attention task) x 2 (modality) mixed ANOVA model for overall recognition (d a ) included the main effect of attention and the interaction (BF10 = 8.04e+10, interaction ƞ p2 = .29, 95% CI [0.12, 0.43]).

This model was 4.45 times more likely than the second-best model, which included both main effects and the interaction. Simple main effects revealed weak evidence of a difference among the three attention conditions for auditory d a (BF10 = 2.93, ƞ p2 = .10, 95% CI [0.00, 0.24]), but decisive evidence in favor of a large difference for visual recognition (BF10 = 9.87e+9, ƞ p2 = .53, 95% CI [0.37, 0.64]). Comparison of the two divided attention tasks revealed weak evidence of a null effect (same vs.

different: auditory BF10 = .34, d = .20, 95% CI [-0.36, 0.75], and visual BF10 = .33, d = .18, 95% CI [-0.38, 0.74]). Collapsing across the divided attention tasks to directly compare divided and full attention groups revealed decisive evidence of a large effect in visual d a (BF10 = 7.32e+10, d = 2.20, 95% CI [1.61, 2.79]), and positive evidence of a medium effect on auditory d a (BF10 = 7.82, d = .69, 95% CI [0.20, 1.18]).

Thus, dividing attention, regardless of the modality of the n-back task, reduced recognition performance in the visual condition more than in the auditory condition.

For recollection, the best ANOVA model included the interaction and both main effects (BF10 = 2.03e+10, interaction ƞ p2 = .24, 95% CI [0.08, 0.39]). This model was 37.22 times more likely than the second-best model. There was negligible evidence of a simple main effect of attention on auditory recollection (BF10 = 1.07, ƞ p2 = .07, 95% CI [0.00, 0.20]), but decisive evidence of a large effect on visual recollection (BF10 = 3.42e+8, ƞ p2 = .48, 95% CI [0.31, 0.60]). Comparison of the two divided attention tasks revealed positive evidence for null effects on auditory (BF10 = .30, d = .11, 95% CI [-0.45, 0.66]) and visual recollection (BF10 = .28, d = .02, 95% CI [-0.53, 0.58]). Collapsing across the divided attention groups and comparing them to the full attention group revealed decisive evidence of a large effect of attention on visual recollection (BF10 = 2.59e+9, d = 2.01, 95% CI [1.43, 2.59]), and weak evidence of a medium effect on auditory recollection (BF10 = 2.85, d = .58, 95% CI [0.09, 1.07]). Thus, dividing attention, regardless of the modality of the task, reduced recollection in the visual condition but it had an ambiguous effect in the auditory condition.

For familiarity, the best ANOVA model included the main effect of attention and the interaction (BF10 = 14,097.46, interaction ƞ p2 = .11, 95% CI [0.01, 0.25]). This model was 4.04 times more likely than the second-best model. There was weak evidence of an effect of attention on auditory familiarity (BF10 = 1.62, ƞ p2 = .09, 95% CI [.00, .21]) and decisive evidence of a large effect on visual familiarity (BF10 = 26,020.62, ƞ p2 = .32, 95% CI [.15, .46]). Comparison of the two divided attention tasks revealed weak evidence of null effects of task type in both auditory (BF10 = .40, d = .26, 95% CI [-.30, .82]) and visual (BF10 = .43, d = .29, 95% CI [-.27, .85]) conditions. Comparing collapsed divided attention groups to the full attention group revealed decisive evidence of a large effect on visual familiarity (BF10 = 114,073.70, d = 1.42, 95% CI [.89, 1.95]), and positive evidence of a medium effect on auditory familiarity (BF10 = 3.51, d = .60, 95% CI [.12, 1.09]). Overall, dividing attention reduced familiarity, and more so in the visual than the auditory condition.

In sum, we replicated the results of Experiment 2: divided attention (regardless of modality of the n-back task) had little to no effect on auditory recollection, but had a large effect on visual recollection. In addition, this is the third experiment showing that modality has a larger effect on recollection than on familiarity (auditory vs. visual in full attention condition: recollection, BF10 = 2,209.56, d = 1.24, 95% CI [0.64, 1.84]; familiarity, BF10 = .58, d = .32, 95% CI [-0.11, 0.76]). conditions. In the Different condition, the n-back numbers were presented in the modality opposite of the stimuli, and in the Same condition they were in the same modality.

Experiment 4: Equating d a

Although the previous experiments demonstrated different effects of modality and attention on recollection and familiarity, d a was always worse in the auditory condition, which raises the possibility that the effects on the process estimates are due in part to overall recognition performance. To check that the modality and attention effects were not specific to situations where auditory recognition is worse than visual, we aimed to equate visual and auditory d a by reducing performance in the visual condition. We presented the images in greyscale for 500 ms at encoding (in both full and divided attention conditions), with no changes to the presentation of the auditory objects (played for 2 s). We used the n-back task in the modality opposite of the study stimuli (like Experiment 2). This study was preregistered: https://osf.io/q56xf/overview.

Method

Participants

A power analysis based on the effect size in Experiment 3 was conducted and indicated a sample of 64 (32 participants in each attention condition). Sixty-four participants (M age = 20.0, 49 women) from the UNLV Psychology Department's subject pool participated in this experiment in return for course credit. Five additional participants took part in the study but their data were excluded from analyses because they failed to understand how to use the response scale (n = 2), had poor performance (d a < .20, n = 2), or did not complete the divided attention task (n = 1).

Materials

The materials were the same as those used in Experiment 1. However, the color images were transformed to greyscale images for this experiment. The n-back task was presented in the opposite modality of the stimuli during the study phase, as in Experiment 2.

Procedure

The procedure for Experiment 4 was the same as the other experiments, except that the visual objects were presented in greyscale and for a duration of 500 ms, with an ISI of 1-s. Attention was manipulated between participants.

Results and Discussion

Accuracy on the n-back task (see Table 1) in the divided attention condition was greater in the visual than the auditory study condition (BF10 = 68.64, Cohen's d = .60, 95% CI [.27, .94]). RTs in the nback task were slower in the visual study condition (BF10 = 1.10e+8, Cohen's d = 1.37, 95% CI [.97, 1.78]).

Data for Experiment 4 are illustrated in Figure 2 (Exp 4). The best 2(attention) x 2(modality) mixed ANOVA model for recognition (d a ) included the main effect of attention and the interaction (BF10 = 1,060,532, interaction ƞ p2 = .21, 95% CI [0.06, 0.38]). This model was 1.92 times more likely than the second-best model, which included both main effects and the interaction. Direct comparison of auditory and visual d a in the full attention condition revealed weak evidence of a null effect (BF10 = .55, Cohen's d = .21, 95% CI [-.06, .49]). Thus, there was no evidence of a difference between the two modalities in the full attention condition.

The best ANOVA model for recollection included the interaction and both main effects (BF10 = 5,082.93, interaction ƞ p2 = .16, 95% CI [0.03, 0.32]). This model was 1.75 times more likely than the second-best model, which included the main effect of attention and the interaction. Two-tailed t-tests provided weak evidence of a null effect of attention on auditory recollection (BF10 = .81, d = .42, 95% CI [-0.09, 0.92]), and decisive evidence of an effect on visual recollection (BF10 = 4,254.36, Cohen's d = 1.27, 95% CI [0.72, 1.82]). This pattern replicates the previous findings of a selective effect of attention on visual recollection; matching recognition performance (d a ) did not change the pattern of modality or attention effects on recollection.

The best ANOVA model of familiarity included both main effects and the interaction (BF10 = 2,992.71, modality ƞ p2 = .13, 95% CI [0.02, 0.30], attention ƞ p2 = .20, 95% CI [0.05, 0.37], interaction ƞ p2 = .08, 95% CI [0.00, 0.24]), with higher familiarity estimates for auditory objects and in the full attention condition. This model was 2.52 times more likely than the second-best model, which included both main effects but no interaction. Thus, the effect of attention on familiarity was greater for visual than auditory objects.

In sum, matching d a between the modalities did not change the overall pattern of results for the process estimates: modality produced a larger effect on recollection than familiarity, and attention still had a large effect on visual recollection; evidence of an effect on auditory recollection was inconclusive (BF10 = .81). Thus, the effects of modality (and attention) are unlikely to be due to overall recognition performance.

Collapsed Data

We unexpectedly found that dividing attention had different effects on recollection in the two modalities. Although there was no credible evidence of an attention effect on auditory recollection in the individual experiments, there was a small but consistent numerical difference. To determine whether this difference would be supported with a larger sample, a post hoc two-tailed independentsamples t-test comparing auditory recollection in the full and divided conditions, collapsed across Experiments 1 -3, produced positive evidence of a small to medium effect of attention (n = 168, BF10 = 7.54, d = .45, 95% CI [0.14, 0.75]; see Figure 4). The same comparison for visual recollection showed decisive evidence of a very large effect (BF10 = 1.48e+17, Cohen's d = 1.63, 95% CI [1.28, 1.99]). A 2 (attention) × 2 (modality) mixed ANOVA on the collapsed recollection estimates favored the model with both main effects and the interaction (BF10 = 3.15e+27, interaction ƞ p2 = .23, 95% CI [0.13, 0.33]), which was 3.34e+7 times more likely than the second-best model, which included the main effect of attention and the interaction. For familiarity, the best model included the main effect of attention and the interaction (BF10 = 2.47e+17, interaction ƞ p2 = .12, 95% CI [0.05, 0.22]) and was 1.74 times more likely than the full model. The main effect of attention was large for both recollection and familiarity (they have identical values up to two decimal places: ƞ p2 = .34, 95% CI [0.23, 0.44]). Thus, it is possible that divided attention decreased auditory recollection, but that effect was vastly smaller than the effect on visual recollection. This possibility will have to be tested with a larger sample size in a single experiment in the future. familiarity estimates on the right. DA = divided attention; FA = full attention. Circles overlying the bars represent individual participants' data points.

Correlations

The findings suggest that the difference in the ability to encode and/or retrieve detail is particularly important for modality effects. We reasoned that if the parameter estimates in the two modalities were based on similar types of memory quality and strength, we should see positive correlations between the auditory and visual conditions for each parameter. If, however, there was a substantial difference in the quality or strength of the memories, we expected to see small or no correlations. We collapsed across the full-attention groups' data in the first three experiments to examine post-hoc correlations between the visual and auditory recollection, and visual and auditory familiarity. There was no clear evidence for or against a correlation between recollection of auditory and visual objects (r(84) = .18, 95% CI [-.03, .38], BF10 = .97; Figure 5A). In contrast, a medium-strength correlation between auditory and visual familiarity estimates was decisively supported (Figure 5A, r(84) = .40, 95% CI [0.21, 0.57], BF10 = 244.66). This pattern suggests that familiarity may be based on similar types of information in the two modalities (i.e., an acontextual strength signal), but that the quality of representations retrieved via recollection differs.

If this relationship is real, then it might be the case that reducing the information available to be encoded (and/or changing the way it can be encoded), would render representations in the two modalities more similar to each other. That is, making the visual task more difficult could change the nature of the representations such that they include fewer contextual details, perhaps similar to the auditory representations. We did this in Experiment 4 by changing the images to greyscale and speeding their presentation during encoding in both the full and divided attention condition 6 . Examining the correlation between auditory and visual process estimates (Figure 5B, full attention conditions only), we found very strong evidence of positive correlations between modalities for both recollection (r(30) = .58, 95% CI [.29, .77], BF10 = 71.10) and familiarity (r(30) = .59, 95% CI [.31, .7,8] BF10 = 93.69). The two recollection correlations (i.e., for the collapsed data and Experiment 4 data) were significantly different 6 Tests with the collapsed data were post-hoc. After preregistration, but before looking at Experiment 4 data, we developed the hypothesis that there would be differences between auditory-visual recollection correlations in previous experiments and in Experiment 4. Thus, the preregistration says that we expected no correlations in recollection based on the previous experiments.

(two-tailed z = -2.22, p = .026), whereas the two familiarity correlations were not (two-tailed z = -1.20, p = .229). Thus, in the full attention condition, reducing encoding of the pictures seems to have rendered the retrieved visual representations more like the auditory representations. familiarity estimates in the two modalities in the full attention conditions across the first three experiments. The evidence of a correlation between recollection estimates in Panel A was inconclusive; however, had the evidence been conclusive, the correlation was estimated at only .18. But there is a moderate correlation for the familiarity estimates. Panel B depicts the same results, but from Experiment 4 only. A moderate correlation emerged between the recollection estimates, and the correlation remained stable for familiarity estimates in comparison to the previous experiments.

General Discussion

Although it has been clear for some time that episodic recognition is better for visual than auditory objects, there is limited theory offering explanations of how and why. Some have suggested differences in precision or fidelity (Gloede & Gregg, 2019) and/or differences in the reliance on gist and verbatim information to explain the modality effect (Ahmad et al., 2024; Zhang et al., 2026). We approached the issue by examining how recognition memory processes were affected by modality and found that the effect on recollection is larger than familiarity. This suggests that the difference between the modalities is largely due to the ability to encode and/or retrieve detail, and that simple memory strength, as indexed by familiarity estimates, plays a more minor role (at least in standard old/new recognition tests). Moreover, correlation analyses showed that there was there was only a weak, inconclusive relationship between auditory and visual recollection (r = .18) until overall recognition (d a ) was matched between the modalities (full attention condition, Experiment 4), in which case a positive relationship emerged.

Reducing the detail and duration of the pictures seemed to render visual recognition more similar to auditory recognition, possibly by reducing the quality of the representation.

This explanation is strengthened by the fact that the correlations between auditory and visual familiarity estimates were consistently positive and did not change (significantly) when d a was matched (i.e., as would be expected of a simple strength signal devoid of qualitative detail).

To better understand the nature of the modality difference in recollection (and following Jacoby's approach to dissociations), we divided attention during the study phases of Experiments 2 -4.

We were surprised to find that the effect size of attention on recollection was much larger for pictures than for sounds, even when data were collapsed across experiments to boost power for easier detection of a small effect. In fact, estimates of recollection in the divided attention visual condition and the full attention auditory condition were similar in Experiment 2; and in Experiments 3 and 4, recollection of visual object under divided attention fell below auditory recollection under full attention. Thus, when sounds were encoded in ideal conditions, auditory retrieval seemed to produce at least as much detail as visual retrieval when pictures were encoded under divided attention.

This unexpected difference in effects makes attention an appealing candidate explanation for modality differences. One possibility, then, is that the role of attention in recognition memory of visual and auditory objects differs. However, encoding was intentional in our experiments, so attention should have been recruited similarly in the two conditions. Another possibility is that there are more attentional lapses during encoding of auditory objects under full attention, such that adding a secondary task has little effect because attention is already divided. However, this idea is challenged by studies of working memory and change detection that indicate that overall performance and semantic effects in auditory and visual object processing are similar (Crutcher & Beer, 2011; Hawkins et al., 2025; Irsik et al., 2016; Vanden Bosch der Nederlanden et al., 2016; Visscher et al., 2007). Thus, attention does not provide an easy explanation of modality differences, but there could be some attention-dependent component of visual recollection that is either absent, less important, or less impervious to insult than auditory recollection.

A second candidate explanation is that visual representations and/or retrieval are, on average, more precise than auditory representations/retrieval, based on performance on similar-lure tests (Ahmad et al., 2024; Gloede & Gregg, 2019). Gloede and Gregg suggested that this could be due to the speed with which visual and auditory features of a stimulus can be perceived and encoded. Participants can sample static visual objects more than dynamic auditory objects, and connecting the stimulus to its meaning may be faster for visual than for auditory objects. If so, there would be more time for encoding, elaboration and/or rehearsal of pictures, and the modality difference could be attributed to functional presentation and rehearsal time differences. Importantly though, we purposefully confounded presentation and ISI time with modality: auditory objects were 2-s long and had a 2.5-s ISI, whereas visual objects (in Experiments 1 -3) appeared on screen for 700 ms, with a 1-s ISI. This confound should ensure that modality effects are not just a function of stimulus duration, but given the fundamental timing differences between auditory and visual processing, it is difficult to rule this out entirely.

Similarly, Ensor et al. (2019) noted that visual stimuli may have an encoding advantage because their features appear simultaneously whereas auditory features necessarily unfold over time. As such, the ability to bind features together may be more difficult for sounds than pictures (but see Bigelow & Poremba, 2014), resulting in poorer access to fine detail at test. Although one might expect that auditory recollection would then be at floor in a divided attention condition, the effects of attention on memory precision have not yet been established in long-term memory to our knowledge.

Another possibility is that auditory and visual objects may differ in their degrees of conceptual and perceptual distinctiveness (feature overlap). Konkle et al. (2010 items was based on participants' subjective judgments. So, it could also be the case that there actually is greater feature overlap among the auditory objects, rendering them less discriminable, even if participants did not detect it, although that seems unlikely. Our stimuli differed from theirs though, so we cannot rule out the possibility that distinctiveness was greater for our visual than auditory stimuli. It will be useful to examine whether controlling distinctiveness eliminates the modality effect. In general, studies of auditory and visual recognition memory have not done much to match the stimuli on various features that we typically would for words, such as frequency or nameability. Additionally, memory of verbs is often more sensitive to context than memory for nouns (e.g., Earles & Kersten, 2017; Kersten & Earles, 2004; Reynolds & Flagg, 1976); that is, sounds are more likely to reflect verbs (e.g., barking) because static objects tend not to make sound, whereas pictures are more likely to depict static objects and therefore reflect nouns (e.g., a vase). A comparison of pictures reflecting verbs (e.g., an image of a dog barking) and sounds will be important. Additionally, the total time of the encoding phases differed across modalities. Although this was necessary to keep visual performance below ceiling, an experiment that holds study time equal across conditions will be needed. In sum, there are still a number of methodological factors to rule out as potential causes of the auditory-visual modality effect.

The finding that divided attention had little effect on auditory recollection but a substantial effect on visual recollection raises questions about how recollection works in general. A tacit assumption of memory theories is that the processes they propose should work similarly across domains, and yet the data presented here suggest that that may not always be the case. Memory models have been routinely built on data from experiments on verbal and visual stimuli, but our findings suggest that we need to include other stimuli too. For instance, is it the case that there is something unique about auditory stimuli that reduces recollection (e.g., different levels of encoded and/or retrieved detail available), and/or could it be that recollection operates somewhat differently in different modalities?

For example, Parks et al. (2011) found that recollection in source memory tasks operated differently depending on the complexity of the stimuli; recollection of more complex stimuli showed more variance than recollection of simple stimuli. This suggests that we should investigate other traditional manipulations (e.g., repetition, spacing, etc.) to see if they affect memory processes similarly (in the same direction and/or to the same extent) for the two modalities.

Given the evidence to date on the effects of divided attention on memory, we were surprised to see that it reduced familiarity estimates. Familiarity suffered under divided attention (main effect attention in collapsed data (F(1, 166) = 86.02, ƞ p2 = .34, 95% CI [0.23, 0.44]) to about the same extent as recollection did (main effect attention in collapsed data (F(1, 166) = 85.32, ƞ p2 = .34, 95% CI [0.23, 0.44];

Fs are presented here because the values are the same at two decimal places). This was not the focus of our experiments and thus we hesitate to interpret it, but it is worth noting that dividing attention with an n-back task might be a fairly extreme manipulation of attention. Most studies on divided attention and memory have used somewhat easier secondary tasks (e.g., detecting three odd digits in a row).

Research on dual-task performance has shown that it is the combination of task demands that determines the extent of performance impairments (e.g., Hirst & Kalamar, 1987), and those effects are greatest when the two tasks rely on the same or highly-overlapping processes. Combining two memory tasks might produce one of the largest divided attention effects on recognition because they depend so heavily on similar processes and resources. Thus, it seems logical that different ways of dividing attention could produce different effects on recognition memory. It will be important to see if our current results replicate when other secondary tasks are used, or when recollection is undermined in other ways. Furthermore, testing a wide range of divided attention tasks to determine whether different secondary tasks have different consequences for memory processes may be warranted. For example, we do not know whether an n-back task would substantially reduce familiarity for words.

A major limitation of studying differences between visual and auditory recognition memory is that they have different temporal characteristics. Auditory objects are inherently dynamic, whereas the visual stimuli we used were stationary and rather simple (colored line drawings). However, some evidence suggests that auditory memory is still inferior to visual memory even when temporal dynamics are controlled and equated in the two modalities (Bigelow & Poremba, 2014). Nonetheless, it would be ideal to compare sounds to silent videos to better equate the stimuli on the temporal dimension.

Similarly, we equated overall recognition in Experiment 4 by reducing recognition of pictures, but it will be important to do it by improving auditory recognition as well.

We have focused on recollection because the modality effect was larger and because of the unexpected effects of dividing attention in the two modalities. However, familiarity differed substantially as well. Images presented for 700 ms produced a stronger familiarity signal than sounds presented for 2 s under full attention in Experiments 1 -3 (collapsed data: BF10 = 163.82, d = .48, 95% CI [0.23, 0.72]). This simple strength difference is interesting in its own right. Future work controlling stimulus features, using different ways of equating overall recognition, and timing are likely needed.

Overall, we showed that the effect of audio-visual modality was much larger on recollection than on familiarity in an old/new item recognition test. Surprisingly, we also found that dividing attention affected auditory and visual recollection differently, which raises a number of questions about recollection and the variables that affect it. The next task is to delve into the nature of those differences.

For example, one question that needs to be answered is whether auditory recognition is worse due to encoding, retrieval, or both. Investigating these questions, the candidate explanations for differences, as well as the neural underpinnings of auditory object memory (e.g., MTL -auditory cortex connections, Billig et al., 2022) will hold some clues about the nature of the difference between memory for visual and auditory objects.

Further examination of the relationship between recognition memory and conceptual and perceptual distinctiveness led the authors to conclude that conceptual distinctiveness lay at the heart of excellent recognition of images. This might suggest that visual superiority is therefore due to conceptual information, but Konkle et al. looked only at visual stimuli. Ahmad et al. (2024) looked at this possibility using a different way of measuring conceptual and perceptual distinctiveness and concluded that the modality difference in their study could not be attributed to one type of distinctiveness more than the other. Thus, there is currently no evidence in the memory literature to support the idea that the visual memory advantage is caused by greater visual distinctiveness, but the questions about the roles of conceptual and perceptual information and feature overlap are still far from resolved. It is important to note that verbatim and gist memory do not perfectly align with recollection and familiarity. In the dual process framework, recollection and familiarity are fundamentally different in that recollection returns qualitative details while familiarity does not. In the fuzzy-trace framework, both verbatim and gist traces can contain qualitative detail, but to different degrees of precision.
